The Benefits of Diligence
Victor Arrial, Giulio Guerrieri, Delia Kesner

Abstract
This paper studies the strength of embedding Call-by-Name ( dCBN ) and Call-by-Value ( dCBV ) into a unifying framework called the Bang Calculus ( dBANG ). These embeddings enable establishing (static and dynamic) properties of dCBN and dCBV through their respective counterparts in 𝚍𝙱𝙰𝙽𝙶 {\tt dBANG} . While some specific static properties have been already successfully studied in the literature, the dynamic ones are more challenging and have been left unexplored. We accomplish that by using a standard embedding for the (easy) dCBN case, while a novel one must be introduced for the (difficult) dCBV case. Moreover, a key point of our approach is the identification of dBANG diligent reduction sequences, which eases the preservation of dynamic properties from 𝚍𝙱𝙰𝙽𝙶 {\tt dBANG} to 𝚍𝙲𝙱𝙽 / 𝚍𝙲𝙱𝚅 {\tt dCBN}/{\tt dCBV} . We illustrate our methodology through two concrete applications: confluence/factorization for both dCBN and dCBV are respectively derived from confluence/factorization for dBANG .



1
Introduction

Call-by-Name (CBN) and Call-by-Value (CBV) stand as two foundational
evaluation strategies inspiring distinct techniques and models of
computation in the theory of programming languages and proof
assistants
[
43
]
. Notably, most theoretical studies in the

\lambda
-calculus still continues to focus on its CBN variant, while
CBV, the cornerstone of operational semantics for most programming
languages and proof assistants, has been less extensively explored.
This is due in particular to the CBV stipulation that an argument can
be passed to a function only when it is a
value
(
i.e.
variable
or abstraction), making the reasoning notably challenging to grasp.
Consequently, some fundamental concepts in the theory of the

\lambda
-calculus (e.g. denotational semantics, contextual
equivalence, solvability, Böhm trees) make subtle –and not entirely
understood– distinctions between CBN and CBV, sometimes resulting in
completely ad-hoc scenarios for CBV, not being uniform with the
corresponding notion in CBN. This is for example the case of CBV Böhm
trees
[
30
]
or the notion of substitution
in
[
47
]
.

Unifying Frameworks.

Reynolds
[
44
]
(quoted by Levy
[
34
]
) advocated
for a unifying framework for CBN and CBV. This not only
minimizes their arbitrariness, but also avoids developing and proving
distinct and independent concepts and properties for them from scratch. Indeed,
both paradigms can be encompassed into broader foundational
frameworks
[
35
,
1
,
16
,
15
,
45
,
37
,
34
,
20
,
22
]

that explicitly differentiate values by marking them with a
distinguished constructor. While multiple such frameworks exist, our
focus lies on the Bang
Calculus
[
21
,
27
,
17
]
.
Inspired by Girard’s Linear Logic (LL)
[
25
]
and Ehrhard’s
interpretation
[
20
]
of Levy’s
Call-by-Push-Value
[
34
]
into LL, the Bang Calculus is
obtained by enriching the
\lambda
-calculus with two distinguished
modalities
\oc
and
\mathsf{der}
. The modality
\oc
plays a twofold
role: it marks what can be duplicated or erased during evaluation (
i.e.
copied an
arbitrary number of times, including zero), and it freezes the
evaluation of subterms (called
thunks
). The modality
\mathsf{der}

annihilates the effect of
\oc
. Embedding CBN or CBV into the Bang
Calculus just consists in decorating
\lambda
-terms with
\oc
and

\mathsf{der}
, thus forcing one model of computation or the other one.
Thanks to these two modalities, the Bang Calculus eases the
identification of shared behaviors and properties of CBN and CBV,
encompassing both syntactic and semantic aspects of them, within a
unifying and simple framework.

Adequate Models of Computation.

Both CBN and CBV were originally defined on
closed
terms
(without occurrences of free variables), that are enough to model
execution of programs. However, evaluation in proof assistants must be
performed on possibly
open
terms, that is, with free variables.
While open terms are harmless to CBN, the theory of the CBV

\lambda
-calculus on open terms turns out to be much more subtle and
trickier
(see
[
6
,
7
,
8
]

for a detailed discussion). In particular, Plotkin’s original
CBV
[
43
]
is not
adequate
for open terms, as there
exist terms that may be both
irreducible
and

meaningless/unsolvable
. The non-adequacy problem in Plotkin’s
CBV calculus can be repaired by introducing a form of sharing
implemented by
explicit substitutions (ES)
, together with a
notion of
reduction at a
distance

[
9
,
10
]
, like in the
Value Substitution Calculus
[
11
]
(here called

dCBV
), a CBV variant of Accattoli and Kesner’s linear substitution
calculus
[
2
,
3
]
(generalizing in turn Milner’s
calculus
[
38
,
32
]
). Adequacy also fails for the version
of the Bang Calculus studied
in
[
27
,
23
]
, for the same
reasons as in CBV. It can be repaired again via ES and distance,
resulting in the Distant Bang Calculus

dBANG

[
17
,
18
]
.
It is then natural to also integrate ES and distance in the CBN
specification: this gives rise to CBN substitution calculi at a distance
[
9
,
10
]
, here we call
dCBN
the one in
[
2
]
, which is adequate as the usual CBN. In summary, we
focus in this paper on a CBN calculus
dCBN
, an adequate CBV
calculus
dCBV
, and the adequate unifying Distant Bang Calculus

dBANG
.

Static and dynamic.

The literature has shown that some
static

properties of CBN and CBV, including normal forms
[
33
]
,
quantitative typing
[
17
]
, tight
typing
[
33
,
18
]
,
inhabitation
[
12
]
, and denotational
semantics
[
27
]
, can be inferred from their
corresponding counterparts in the (Distant) Bang Calculus by
exploiting suitable CBN and CBV encodings. However, retrieving

dynamic
properties from the Bang Calculus into CBN or CBV turns
out to be a more intricate task, especially in their
adequate

(distant)
variant
[
27
,
17
,
23
,
18
]
.
Indeed, it is easy to obtain
simulation
(a CBN or CBV reduction sequence is always embedded into a
dBANG
reduction sequence),
but
the
converse, known as
reverse simulation
, fails: a
dBANG
reduction sequence from a term
in the image of the CBN or CBV embedding may not correspond to a
valid reduction sequence in CBN or CBV (counterexample in

Fig.

1
).
Up to these days, there are no embeddings in the
literature enjoying reverse simulation for an adequate CBV calculus,
so that it is not possible to export
dynamic properties from
dBANG
to both
dCBN
and
dCBV

.

Contributions.

We first revisit and
extend
the
existing static and dynamic preservation results relating
dCBN
and

dBANG
,
including simulation and reverse simulation, exploiting the embedding
used in

[
17
,
18
]
.
However, our primary and most significant contribution is a new

methodology
to deal with the (adequate) calculus
dCBV
.
Indeed, we define a
novel embedding
from
dCBV
into

dBANG
,
refining
the one of

[
17
,
18
]
, that
finely decorates terms with the modalities
\oc
and
\mathsf{der}
.
To avoid redundant decorations, as
\oc
and
\mathsf{der}
annihilate each other, a dedicated

{\tt d\oc}
-reduction step is then applied
on the fly
by the
embedding, as in
[
17
,
18
]
. But our new

dCBV
embedding not only preserves static and dynamic properties,
but also satisfies
reverse simulation
, an essential property
that was previously lacking. This achievement is realized by the
second ingredient of our new methodology, given by the notion of

diligent sequence
in
dBANG
, a concept standing
independently of the embeddings. Indeed, a challenge at this point is
to prove that the earlier mentioned
{\tt d\oc}
-reductions have a
purely
administrative
nature, and additionally, that they can
be treated
diligently
, by executing all of them as soon as
possible. We call this method
diligent administration
: we
consistently address all administrative steps before proceeding with
any other
computational
steps. A further challenge is then to establish that
working with administrative diligence does not alter the CBN or CBV

nature of evaluation
.

As explained above, reverse simulation is crucial to derive properties
for
dCBN
and
dCBV
from their respective properties in

dBANG
. We provide two main illustrative
applications
of
this by studying the cases of
confluence
and

factorization
. Confluence is a well known property, and
factorization is crucial to prove important results in
rewriting
[
39
,
14
,
48
,
50
,
2
,
4
,
5
,
29
,
26
,
24
]
:
we say that a reduction enjoys factorization when every reduction
sequence can be rearranged so that some specific relevant steps are
performed first. In the two last sections, we use
confluence/factorization for
dBANG
as a basis to easily deduce
confluence/factorization for
dCBN
and
dCBV
. This is done by
exploiting the CBN and CBV embeddings back and forth, via reduction
simulation and reverse simulation. Just one proof is enough for three
confluence/factorization results: it’s a three-for-one deal!
The fact that
dCBN
and
dCBV
confluence/factorizations can be

easily
derived from
dBANG
confluence/factorization in
essentially the
same
way is another achievement, attained
thanks to having introduced good tools, such as diligence and the new

dCBV
embedding.

We actually provide a first proof of factorization for
dBANG
,
another major contribution of this paper. Factorizations in
dCBN
and
dCBV
were already proved in
[
2
]

and
[
11
]
, respectively, but their proofs are not
trivial, even when applying some abstract approach
[
2
]
.

Road Map.

Sec.

2
recalls
dBANG
and introduces
diligence. The
dCBN
/
dCBV
calculi and their embeddings are
presented in
Sec.

3
, together with their
corresponding (static and dynamic) preservation results.

Sec.

4
derives
dCBN
/
dCBV
confluence from that of
dBANG
.
Sec.

5
proves a
factorization result for
dBANG
, and deduces factorization for

dCBN
and
dCBV
by projection.
Sec.

6
discusses
future and related work and concludes. Proofs can be found
in the appendix.

1.1
Basic Notions Used all along the Paper.

An
abstract rewriting system (
ARS
)

\mathcal{E}
is a set
E

with a binary relation
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
on
E
, called
reduction
. We
write
u\;{}_{\mathcal{E}}\!\!\leftarrow t
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}u
, and we denote by
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{+}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
) the transitive (resp. reflexive-transitive) closure of

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
. Given
t\in E
,
t
is an
\mathcal{E}
-normal form

(
\mathcal{E}
-
NF
) if there is no
u\in E
such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}u
;
t

is
\mathcal{E}
-terminating
if there is no infinite
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}

reduction sequence starting at
t
. Reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
is

terminating
if every
t\in E
is
\mathcal{E}
-terminating;

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
is
diamond
if for any
t,u_{1},u_{2}\in E
such that

u_{1}\;{}_{\mathcal{E}}\!\!\leftarrow t\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}u_{2}
and
u_{1}\neq u_{2}
, there is
s\in E
such
that
u_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}s\;{}_{\mathcal{E}}\!\!\leftarrow u_{2}
;
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
is
confluent
if

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
is diamond.

All reductions in this paper will be defined by a set of rewrite rules

\mathbb{R}
, closed by a set of contexts
\mathbb{E}
. A term being an instance of the left-hand side of a rewrite rule

\mathcal{R}\in\mathbb{R}
is called a
\mathcal{R}
-redex
. Given a
rule
\mathcal{R}\in\mathbb{R}
, and a context
{\tt E}\in\mathbb{E}
, we use
\rightarrow_{{\tt E}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}
to denote the reduction of the

\mathcal{R}
-redex under the context
{\tt E}
. The reduction

\rightarrow_{\mathbb{E}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}
is the union of reductions
\rightarrow_{{\tt E}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}
over

all
contexts
{\tt E}\in\mathbb{E}
. In other words,

\rightarrow_{\mathbb{E}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}
is the closure of the rule
\mathcal{R}
under all the
contexts in
\mathbb{E}
.

2
The Distant Bang Calculus
dBANG

We introduce the term syntax of

dBANG

[
17
]
. Given a countably
infinite set
\mathcal{X}
of variables
x,y,z,\dots
, the set

\Lambda_{\oc}
of terms is defined inductively as follows:

\textbf{(Terms)}\qquad t,u,s\;\Coloneqq\;x\in\mathcal{X}\;|\;tu\;|\;\lambda x.t\;|\;\oc t\;|\;\mathsf{der}(t)\;|\;t[x\backslash u]

The set
\Lambda_{\oc}
includes
variables

x
,

abstractions

\lambda x.t
,
applications

tu
,

closures

t[x\backslash u]
representing a pending
explicit
substitution
(
ES
)
[x\backslash u]
on
t
,
bangs

\oc t

and
derelictions

\mathsf{der}(t)
(their operational meaning is

explained below
).

Abstractions
\lambda x.t
and closures
t[x\backslash u]
bind the
variable
x
in their body
t
. The set of
free variables

{\tt fv}(t)
of a term
t
is defined as expected, in particular

{\tt fv}(\lambda x.t)\coloneqq{\tt fv}(t)\setminus\{x\}
and

{\tt fv}(t[x\backslash u])\coloneqq{\tt fv}(u)\cup({\tt fv}(t)\setminus\{x\})
. The usual notion of
\alpha
-conversion

[
14
]
is extended to the whole set
\Lambda_{\oc}
, and
terms are identified up to
\alpha
-conversion,
e.g.

y[y\backslash\lambda x.x]=z[z\backslash\lambda y.y]
. We denote by

t\{x\backslash u\}
the usual (capture avoiding) meta-level substitution of

u
for all free occurrences of
x
in
t
.

Full contexts

({\tt F}\in\mathbb{F})
,

surface contexts

({\tt S}\in\mathbb{S})
and

list contexts

({\tt L}\in\mathbb{L})
, which can
be seen as terms with exactly one
hole

\diamond
, are
inductively defined by:

(Full Contexts)

\displaystyle{\tt F}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;{\tt F}\,t\;|\;t\,{\tt F}\;|\;\lambda x.{\tt F}\;|\;\oc{\tt F}\;|\;\mathsf{der}({\tt F})\;|\;{\tt F}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash{\tt F}]

(Surface Contexts)

\displaystyle{\tt S}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;{\tt S}\,t\;|\;t\,{\tt S}\;|\;\lambda x.{\tt S}\;|\;\mathsf{der}({\tt S})\;|\;{\tt S}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash{\tt S}]

(List Contexts)

\displaystyle{\tt L}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;{\tt L}[x\backslash t]

{\tt L}
and
{\tt S}
are special cases of
{\tt F}
: the
hole may occur everywhere in
{\tt F}
, while in
{\tt S}
it
cannot appear under a
\oc
. List contexts
{\tt L}
are arbitrary
lists of ES, used to implement reduction at a
distance
[
9
,
10
]
. We write

{\tt F}\!\left<t\right>
for the term obtained by replacing the hole in

{\tt F}
with the term
t
(possibly capturing the free
variables of
t
).

The following
rewrite rules
are the base components of
our reductions.

\displaystyle{\tt L}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>u

\displaystyle\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}{\tt L}\!\left<t[x\backslash u]\right>

\displaystyle t[x\backslash{\tt L}\!\left<\oc u\right>]

\displaystyle\mapsto_{{\tt s\oc}}{\tt L}\!\left<t\{x\backslash u\}\right>

\displaystyle\mathsf{der}({\tt L}\!\left<\oc t\right>)

\displaystyle\mapsto_{{\tt d\oc}}{\tt L}\!\left<t\right>

Rule
{\tt dB}
(resp.
{\tt s\oc}
) is assumed to be
capture-free, so no free variable of
u
(resp.
t
) is captured by
the context
{\tt L}
.
The rule
{\tt dB}
fires a
\beta
-redex, generating an ES. The
rule
{\tt s\oc}
fires an ES provided that its argument is
duplicable,
i.e.
is a bang. The rule
{\tt d\oc}
uses
\mathsf{der}
to
erase a
\oc
. In all of these rewrite rules, the reduction acts

at a distance

[
9
,
10
]
: the
main constructors involved in the rule can be separated by a finite
—possibly empty— list
{\tt L}
of ES. This mechanism unblocks
desired computations that otherwise would be stuck,
e.g.

(\lambda x.x)[y\backslash w]\oc z\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}x[x\backslash\oc z][y\backslash w]
.

Reductions are defined, as specified in
Sec.

1.1
, by
taking the set of rewrite rules
\{{\tt dB},{\tt s\oc},{\tt d\oc}\}
and the sets of contexts
\mathbb{S}
and

\mathbb{F}
.
Surface reduction
is the relation

\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
, while
full reduction
is the
relation
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
. For example, for

{\tt S}_{1}=\diamond\in\mathbb{S}
and
{\tt F}_{1}=\oc\diamond\in\mathbb{F}\setminus\mathbb{S}
:
(\lambda x.\oc\mathsf{der}(\oc x))\oc y\;\rightarrow_{{\tt S}_{1}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\;(\oc\mathsf{der}(\oc x))[x\backslash\oc y]\;\rightarrow_{{\tt S}_{1}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\;\oc\mathsf{der}(\oc y)\;\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{1}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}\;\oc y
. The
first two steps are
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}
- and also
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}
-steps,
while the last one is a
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}
-step but not a

\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}
-step. More generally,
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}\,\subsetneq\,\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}
. For instance,
\oc(\mathsf{der}(\oc y))
is a
\mathbb{S}
-
NF
but
not a
\mathbb{F}
-
NF
since
\oc(\mathsf{der}(\oc y))\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}\oc y
,
while
\oc y
is a
\mathbb{F}
-
NF
(and hence a
\mathbb{S}
-
NF
too).

The
\oc
modality plays a twofold role. First, it marks the only
subterms that can be substituted (
i.e.
erased or arbitrarily copied):
the
{\tt s\oc}
-rule fires an ES only if there is a
\oc
in its
argument (up to a list context). Second, it freezes (surface)
evaluation of the term under the scope of
\oc
: surface reduction

\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}
does not reduce under
\oc
. In full reduction

\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}
, the
\oc
modality looses its freezing behavior.

Diligent Administration.

While reductions
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}
are
actual
computational
steps, reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
is
rather
administrative
in nature. As we use
dBANG
to
simulate other calculi, we need to align with the
implicit
nature
of these administrative steps: this can be achieved by
executing them as soon as possible. We thus introduce a
diligent
process
that reorders some reduction steps to ensure that
administrative steps are always performed as soon as
there is a

{\tt d\oc}
-redex
.

To begin, we formally introduce the concept of
diligent
administrative
reduction sequence, characterizing sequences where
each
computational
step (
{\tt dB}
or
{\tt s\oc}
)
can be performed only
after
all
administrative
steps
(
{\tt d\oc}
) have been executed.

Definition 1
(Diligent Administrative Reduction)

The
diligent administrative

surface
(resp.
full
) reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}{\tt ad}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}{\tt ad}}
) is a subset of the surface (resp. full)
reduction obtained by restricting
{\tt dB}
- and

{\tt s\oc}
-steps to
\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>
-normal
forms (resp.
\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>
-normal forms). More
precisely, it is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}{\tt ad}}&\coloneqq&(\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cap\;\text{$\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!$-{\tt NF} }\times\Lambda_{\oc})\;\;\cup\;\;(\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\cap\;\text{$\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!$-{\tt NF} }\times\Lambda_{\oc})\;\;\cup\;\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}\\[5.69046pt]
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}{\tt ad}}&\coloneqq&(\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cap\;\text{$\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!$-{\tt NF} }\times\Lambda_{\oc})\;\;\cup\;\;(\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\cap\;\text{$\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!$-{\tt NF} }\times\Lambda_{\oc})\;\;\cup\;\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}\end{array}

Example 1

Consider the two surface reduction sequences
\mathsf{der}(\oc x)[x\backslash\oc y]\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\mathsf{der}(\oc y)\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}y
and
\mathsf{der}(\oc x)[x\backslash\oc y]\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}x[x\backslash\oc y]\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}y
. The first one is not diligent
administrative, as the step
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}
is performed in a
term that is not
\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
. But the second one is diligent
administrative:
\mathsf{der}(\oc x)[x\backslash\oc y]\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}{\tt ad}}x[x\backslash\oc y]\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}{\tt ad}}y
.

To show that every reduction sequence can be transformed into a
diligent one (
Lem.

1
), we first observe that it is
possible to perform
all
administrative steps from any term:
indeed, reductions
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
are

terminating
, because each administrative step erase two
constructors,
\mathsf{der}
and
\oc
, so the term size decreases.

Some reduction sequences can be made diligent, as in

Ex.

1
, but this is not the case for all reduction
sequences. For instance
\mathsf{der}(\oc x)[x\backslash\oc y]\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}\mathsf{der}(\oc y)
but
\mathsf{der}(\oc x)[x\backslash\oc y]\not\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}{\tt ad}}\mathsf{der}(\oc y)
. Therefore, we focus solely on reduction sequences
reaching terms that are normal for
{\tt d\oc}
. Under these conditions and by
commuting computational steps with administrative ones, we obtain the
following results:

Lemma 1
(Diligence Process)

\downarrow
Let
t,u\in\Lambda_{\oc}
be terms.

•

(Surface)
If
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}}u
and
u
is a
\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
, then
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}{\tt ad}}u
.

•

(Full)
If
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}}u
and
u
is a
\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
, then
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}{\tt ad}}u
.

3
Call-by-Name and Call-by-Value Embeddings

In this section we present the call-by-name
dCBN
(
Sec.

3.1
) and call-by-value

dCBV
(
Sec.

3.2
) calculi,
as well as their embeddings into
dBANG
, which preserve static
properties (Corollaries
1
.2 and
2
.2 for
dCBN
,
3
.2
and
4
.2 for
dCBV
) and dynamic ones
(Corollaries
1
.3 and
2
.3 for
dCBN
,
3
.3 and

4
.3 for
dCBV
).

Both
dCBN

[
9
,
10
,
2
]

and
dCBV

[
11
]
are specified using ES and
action at a distance, as explained in
Sec.

1
, and they share
the same term syntax. The sets
\Lambda
of
terms
and

\Upsilon
of
values
are inductively defined below.

\textbf{(Terms)}\ \ t,u\;\Coloneqq\;v\;|\;t\,u\;|\;t[x\backslash u]\hskip 28.45274pt\textbf{(Values)}\ \ v\;\Coloneqq\;x\;|\;\lambda x.t

Note that the syntax contains neither
\mathsf{der}
nor
\oc
. The
distinction between terms and values is irrelevant in
dCBN
but
crucial in
dCBV
. The two calculi also share the same

full contexts

{\tt F}
and
list contexts

{\tt L}
, which can be seen as terms with exactly one

hole

\diamond
and are inductively defined below. The
differences between
dCBN
and
dCBV
are in the definitions of

surface
contexts and
rewrite rules
.

(List Contexts)

\displaystyle{\tt L}

\displaystyle\;\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;{\tt L}[x\backslash t]

(Full Contexts)

\displaystyle{\tt F}

\displaystyle\;\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;{\tt F}\,t\;|\;t\,{\tt F}\;|\;\lambda x.{\tt F}\;|\;{\tt F}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash{\tt F}]

3.1
The Call-by-Name Calculus
dCBN
and its embedding to
dBANG

In
dCBN
,
surface contexts

{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}

are defined below: the hole cannot be in the argument of an application or ES.
To align the notations, in
dCBN
full contexts
are denoted by
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
and list contexts by

{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\in\mathbb{L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
.

(
dCBN
Surface Contexts)

\displaystyle{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}

\displaystyle\;\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\,t\;|\;\lambda x.{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\;|\;{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}[x\backslash t]

As explained in
Sec.

1.1
, reductions
in
{\tt dCBN}
are defined by taking the set of rewrite rules

\{{\tt dB},{\tt s}\}
defined below and the sets of contexts

\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
and
\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
.

{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>u\;\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\;{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<t[x\backslash u]\right>\hskip 56.9055ptt[x\backslash u]\;\mapsto_{{\tt s}}\;t\{x\backslash u\}

Rule
dB
is capture-free: no free variable of
u
is captured
by the context
{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
. The
dCBN
surface reduction

is the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt s}\right>}
, while the
dCBN
full reduction
is
the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt s}\right>}
.
E.g.
, for
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}=\lambda z.\diamond
,
t_{0}=\lambda z.((\lambda x.yxx)(zz))\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}t_{1}=\lambda z.((yxx)[x\backslash zz])\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt s}\right>}t_{2}=\lambda z.(y(zz)(zz))
.

The
dCBN
surface reduction is nothing but (a non-deterministic
but diamond variant of) the well-known
head
reduction.

Embedding
dCBN
into
dBANG
.

The
dCBN
embedding

\cdot^{\tt n}\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\oc}
from
dCBN
to
dBANG
, introduced
in
[
17
,
18
]
and
presented below, extends Girard’s one
[
21
]
to ES.

\displaystyle x^{\tt n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq x

\displaystyle(\lambda x.t)^{\tt n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lambda x.t^{\tt n}

\displaystyle(tu)^{\tt n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq t^{\tt n}\,\oc u^{\tt n}

\displaystyle(t[x\backslash u])^{\tt n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq t^{\tt n}[x\backslash\oc u^{\tt n}].

As an example,
(yx)[y\backslash z]^{\tt n}=(y\oc x)[y\backslash\oc z]
. Note that
\cdot^{\tt n}
never
introduces
\mathsf{der}
, hence
t^{\tt n}
, and every term it
reduces to, are always a
\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
(this does not hold for the

dCBV
embedding,

Sec.

3.2
). In every
application and ES,
\cdot^{\tt n}
puts a
\oc
in front of
their argument, which shows the two roles—called

duplicability
and
accessibility
—played by
\oc
in
this embedding:
dCBN
duplicability means that any argument can be
duplicated (or erased),
dCBN
accessibility means that surface reduction cannot take place inside arguments. Indeed, the
\oc
seals
all subterms in argument position.

The embedding is trivially
extended to
dCBN
contexts by setting
\diamond^{\tt n}=\diamond
.

The static properties of this embedding have already been partially
discussed in

[
17
,
18
]
. We
will revisit and refine them (
Cors.

1
,
2
and
8
), but our main focus lies in the
preservation of the dynamics of
dCBN
within
dBANG
. For that,
we first extend the embedding to rule names, by defining

{\tt dB}^{\tt n}\;\coloneqq\;{\tt dB}
and

{\tt s}^{\tt n}\;\coloneqq\;{\tt s\oc}
.

The reduction of a
dCBN
redex can be effectively simulated in

dBANG
by reducing the corresponding redex occurring at the
translated location/context.

Lemma 2
(
dCBN
One-Step Simulation)

Let
t,u\in\Lambda
and
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
and
\mathcal{R}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt s}\}
. If
t\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}u
then
t^{\tt n}\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}\!\left<\mathcal{R}^{\tt n}\right>}u^{\tt n}
.

Example 2

Consider the
dCBN
reductions
t_{0}\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}t_{1}
and
t_{1}\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt s}\right>}t_{2}
seen above with
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}=\lambda z.\diamond
.
Since
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}=\lambda z.\diamond
,
we have

t_{0}^{\tt n}=\lambda z.((\lambda x.y\oc x\oc x)\oc(z\oc z))\allowbreak\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\lambda z.((y\,\oc x\oc x)[x\backslash\oc(z\oc z)])=t_{1}^{\tt n}

and

t_{1}^{\tt n}\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\lambda z.(y\oc(z\oc z)\oc(z\oc z))=t_{2}^{\tt n}
.

So, every
dCBN
reduction step is simulated by the corresponding
dBANG
reduction
step, without the need for any administrative step.
Simulation of
{\tt dCBV}
(
Lem.

5
) is instead more involved, requiring some further administrative steps.

The following property, which effectively reverses the simulation
process, extends the one holding for the original Bang Calculus
(without distance)
[
27
]
.

Lemma 3
(
dCBN
One-Step Reverse Simulation)

Let
t\in\Lambda
,
u^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\oc}
,
{\tt F}\in\mathbb{F}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt s\oc},{\tt d\oc}\}
.

t^{\tt n}\rightarrow_{{\tt F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right>}u^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\hspace{.2cm}}l}\exists\;u\in\Lambda,\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&u^{\tt n}=u^{\prime}\\
\exists\;\mathcal{R}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt s}\},\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&\mathcal{R}^{\tt n}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\\
\exists\;{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}},\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}={\tt F}\end{array}\right\}\text{ such that }t\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}u.

Lem.

3
states that any
dBANG
step from the image
t^{\tt n}
of a
dCBN
term
t
(which
is necessarily diligent, because
t^{\tt n}
is a
{\tt F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
)
actually simulates a
dCBN
step from
t
.
In
Ex.

2
,
t_{0}^{\tt n}

{\tt dB}
-reduces in the context
{\tt F}=\lambda z.\diamond

to
\lambda z.((y\,\oc x\oc x)[x\backslash\oc(z\oc z)])
, which is indeed equal to
t_{1}^{\tt n}
,
and
t_{0}\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}t_{1}
in the context
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}=\lambda z.\diamond
as
well, with
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}={\tt F}
. Note that

Lem.

3
is vacuously true for
\mathcal{R}={\tt d\oc}
, since there is no term
t
such that
\mathsf{der}

occurs in
t^{\tt n}
.

Lems.

2
and
3
have some
significant consequences:

Corollary 1

Let
t,u\in\Lambda
and
s^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\oc}
.

1.

(Stability)
: if
t^{\tt n}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}}s^{\prime}
then there is
s\in\Lambda
such that

s^{\tt n}=s^{\prime}
.

2.

(Normal Forms)
:
t
is a
\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
-
NF
if and only if

t^{\tt n}
is a
\mathbb{F}
-
NF
.

3.

(Simulations)
:
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}}u
if and only if

t^{\tt n}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}}u^{\tt n}
. Moreover,
the number of
{\tt dB}/{\tt s}
-steps on the left
matches the number
{\tt dB}/{\tt s\oc}
-steps on the
right.

These results deserve some comments. Point 1 states that the image of
the
dCBN
embedding is
stable under reduction
. However,
it is not stable under expansion. For
instance,
\mathsf{der}(\oc x)\rightarrow_{{\tt S}}x=x^{\tt n}
,
although
\mathsf{der}(\oc x)
does not belong to the embedding’s image, which
only contains terms without
\mathsf{der}
. Point 2 guarantees the
preservation of normal forms
in both
directions. Finally, Point 3 concerns the
preservation of
reduction sequences
. It is worth highlighting that this is an
equivalence, enabling to inject reduction sequences from
dCBN
into

dBANG
and project them back from
dBANG
into
dCBN
. This is
a key property allowing in particular to infer confluence and factorization
for
{\tt dCBN}
from that for
{\tt dBANG}
.

The reader may wonder whether similar preservation results hold for
surface reduction. Since it is a subreduction of full reduction,

Cor.

1
.1 already implies stability for surface reduction. However, it does not imply preservation of surface normal
forms, and only yields back and forth simulation of surface reduction
via full reduction, which is not exactly what we want:

t^{\tt n}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}}u^{\tt n}
if
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}}u
, and
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}}u
if
t^{\tt n}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}}u^{\tt n}
. So let us come back to analyze the situation for
the
one-step
simulation and reverse simulation. Since surface contexts are special cases of full contexts, then
t\rightarrow_{{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}u

implies
t^{\tt n}\rightarrow_{{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}\!\left<R^{\tt n}\right>}u^{\tt n}
by
Lem.

2
. To prove that this
simulating step is actually a surface step, we need an additional
property: that
dCBN
surface contexts are translated into

dBANG
surface contexts (
Lem.

4
.1). A more subtle analysis will be required for surface reverse
simulation: positions of
dBANG
surface
redexes
are
always in the image of
dCBN
surface contexts:

Lemma 4

1.

(
dCBN

\rightarrow

dBANG
)
If
{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
, then

{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}\in\mathbb{S}
.

2.

(
dBANG

\rightarrow

dCBN
)
If
{\tt S}\in\mathbb{S}
and
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
such that
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}^{\tt n}={\tt S}
, then
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
.

Thanks to
Lem.

4
, one-step simulation
and reverse simulation
(
Lems.

2
and
3
) can be iterated
to obtain the following results about
surface
reduction.

Corollary 2

Let
t,u\in\Lambda
and
s^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\oc}
.

1.

(Stability)
: if
t^{\tt n}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}}s^{\prime}
then there is
s\in\Lambda
such that

s^{\tt n}=s^{\prime}
.

2.

(Normal Forms)
:
t\text{ is a $\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}$-{\tt NF} }
if and
only if
t^{\tt n}
is a
\mathbb{S}
-
NF
.

3.

(Simulations)
:
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}}u
if and only if

t^{\tt n}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}}u^{\tt n}
. Moreover,
the number of
{\tt dB}/{\tt s}
-steps on the left
matches the number of
{\tt dB}/{\tt s\oc}
-steps on
the right.

Our results for
dCBN
notably extend the ones in

[
17
,
18
]
, where
it was only shown that
\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}
-
NF
translates to
\mathbb{S}
-
NF
, and that

dCBN
surface reduction is simulated by
dBANG
surface
reduction: we went further by encompassing their converses.

3.2
The Call-by-Value Calculus
dCBV
and its embedding into
dBANG

In
dCBV
,
surface contexts

{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\in\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}

are defined below: the hole cannot be under an abstraction. To align the notations, in
dCBV
full contexts
are denoted by
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}
and list contexts by

{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\in\mathbb{L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}
.

(
dCBV
Surface Contexts)

\displaystyle{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}

\displaystyle\;\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\,t\;|\;t\,{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\;|\;{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}]

As explained in
Sec.

1.1
, reductions in
{\tt dCBV}
are
defined by taking the set of rewrite rules
\{{\tt dB},{\tt sV}\}
defined below and the sets of contexts
\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}

and
\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}
.

{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<\lambda x.t\right>u\;\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\;{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<t[x\backslash u]\right>\hskip 56.9055ptt[x\backslash{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<v\right>]\;\mapsto_{{\tt sV}}\;{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<t\{x\backslash v\}\right>

Rule
dB
(resp.
sV
) is capture-free: no free
variable of
u
(resp.
t
) is captured by context
{\tt L}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}
. The

dCBV
surface reduction
is the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<{\tt sV}\right>}
, while the

dCBV
full reduction
is the relation
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<{\tt sV}\right>}
.

The calculi
dCBN
and
dCBV
differ in that
dCBN
can always
fire an ES (rule
{\tt s}
), while
dCBV
only does when the ES
argument is a value, possibly wrapped by a finite list of ES (rule

{\tt sV}
). So
e.g.
, for
{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}=(yxx)[x\backslash\diamond]
, we have:

\begin{split}u_{0}&=(\lambda x.yxx)((\lambda z.z)y)\rightarrow_{\diamond\,\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}u_{1}=(yxx)[x\backslash(\lambda z.z)y]\\
&\rightarrow_{{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}u^{\prime}_{1}=(yxx)[x\backslash z[z\backslash y]]\rightarrow_{{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<{\tt sV}\right>}u_{2}=(yxx)[x\backslash y]\rightarrow_{\diamond\,\!\left<{\tt sV}\right>}u_{3}=yyy\end{split}

(1)

Notice how reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}
unblocks
redexes: given
\delta\coloneqq\lambda z.zz
, the term

t\coloneqq(\lambda y.\delta)(xx)\delta
, which is
a normal form
in Plotkin’s CBV
[
43
]
, is now non-terminating

t\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}\delta[y\backslash xx]\delta\allowbreak\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}(zz)[z\backslash\delta][y\backslash xx]\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}(\delta\delta)[y\backslash xx]\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}(\delta\delta)[y\backslash xx]
, as one
would expect, since
t
is semantically equivalent to the diverging
term

\delta\delta

[
41
,
42
,
19
,
6
,
8
]
.

The
dCBV
surface reduction is nothing but the well-known

weak
reduction that does not evaluate under abstractions.

Embedding
dCBV
into
dBANG
.

Values (
i.e.
, variables and abstractions) are the duplicable elements
of
dCBV
. Girard’s Call-by-Value encoding (used in

[
21
,
27
]
and noted

(\cdot)^{{\tt v_{1}}}
here) is built upon this insight, placing a bang
in front of each variable
x^{{\tt v_{1}}}=\oc x
and abstraction

(\lambda x.t)^{{\tt v_{1}}}=\oc\lambda x.t^{{\tt v_{1}}}
. The encoding
of an application is
(tu)^{{\tt v_{1}}}=\mathsf{der}(t^{{\tt v_{1}}})u^{{\tt v_{1}}}
, where the
\mathsf{der}
is
used to enable a
{\tt d\oc}
-step if
t
(the left-hand side of
the application) is a value, so as to restore its functional role.
However, as highlighted in

[
17
,
18
]
, such a
definition fails normal forms preservation: a
dCBV
normal form is
not necessarily encoded by a
dBANG
normal form, for example given
the normal term
t_{0}=xy
we have
t_{0}^{{\tt v_{1}}}=\mathsf{der}(\oc x)\,\oc y
which is not normal. Consequently,

[
17
,
18
]

proposed an alternative encoding (noted
(\cdot)^{{\tt v_{2}}}
here,
whose details are omitted for lack of space), based on the same
principle, but with an additional
super-development
: all
{\tt d\oc}
-redexes appearing during the encoding on the
left of an application are eliminated
on the fly
, so that the embedding
(\cdot)^{{\tt v_{2}}}
preserves normal forms (
e.g.
,
t_{0}^{{\tt v_{2}}}=x\,\oc y
, which is normal in
dBANG
).
But, as shown in
Fig.

1
,
(\cdot)^{{\tt v_{2}}}
breaks reverse simulation with respect to surface reduction.

\begin{array}[]{ccc}(\lambda x.(\lambda y.y)z)z&\quad\not\rightarrow_{{\tt S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}&(\lambda x.y[y\backslash z])z\\[1.42271pt]
\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}\;\cdot^{{\tt v_{2}}}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{-90.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}\;\cdot^{{\tt v_{2}}}\\[0.0pt]
(\lambda x.(\lambda y.\oc y)\oc z)\oc z&\quad\rightarrow_{{\tt S}}&(\lambda x.(\oc y)[y\backslash\oc z])\oc z\end{array}

Figure 1
:
Counterexample to
dCBV
reverse simulation using
the embedding
\cdot^{{\tt v_{2}}}

We introduce a
new

dCBV
embedding that preserves normal
forms and fulfills simulation
and
reverse simulation (this is
one of our main contributions).

Definition 2

The
dCBV
embedding

\cdot^{\tt v}\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\oc}
is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rlp{.3cm}rlll}x^{\tt v}&\coloneqq\oc x&&\hbox{\multirowsetup$(tu)^{\tt v}$}&\hbox{\multirowsetup$\coloneqq\bigg\{$}&\mathsf{der}({\tt L}\!\left<s\right>u^{\tt v})&\text{ if }t^{\tt v}={\tt L}\!\left<\oc s\right>\\
(\lambda x.t)^{\tt v}&\coloneqq\oc\lambda x.\oc t^{\tt v}&&&&\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(t^{\tt v})\,u^{\tt v})&\text{ otherwise;}\\
(t[x\backslash u])^{\tt v}&\coloneqq t^{\tt v}[x\backslash u^{\tt v}].&\end{array}

Note in particular that, thanks to super-development,

t^{\tt v}
is always a
\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
.
For instance,
(\lambda z.z)^{\tt v}=\oc\lambda z.\oc\oc z
and
(yxx)^{\tt v}=\mathsf{der}\big(\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(y\oc x))\oc x\big)
,
whereas

((\lambda x.yxx)(II))^{\tt v}=\mathsf{der}\big(\big(\lambda x.\oc\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(y\oc x))\oc x)\big)\,\mathsf{der}((\lambda z.\oc\oc z)\oc\lambda z.\oc\oc z)\big).

As in the
dCBN
embedding, the modality
\oc
plays a
twofold
role
in our new
dCBV
embedding.
First,
\cdot^{\tt v}
marks with
\oc
subterms to be considered as
values,
i.e.
potentially
erasable
or
duplicable
. This induces the use
of super-developments in the case of applications to avoid some
administrative steps that would otherwise affect preservation of
normal forms. Second,
\cdot^{\tt v}
marks the positions where
surface reduction must not occur: inside values; thus it introduces a
second
(internal)
\oc
in the encoding of abstractions
to encapsulate its body and shield it from surface computation.
Additionally, to restore access to the abstraction’s body when it is
applied, a second (external)
\mathsf{der}
is added to the encoding of
applications. These two principles highlights the dual role of
\oc
in
dBANG
: enabling duplication (and erasure) as well as isolating subterms
from surface computation processes.

The
dCBV
embedding is extended to rule names, by defining

{\tt dB}^{\tt v}\coloneqq{\tt dB}
and

{\tt sV}^{\tt v}\coloneqq{\tt s\oc}
.
Similarly to
dCBN
, we have the fundamental simulation result below.

Lemma 5
(
dCBV
One-Step Simulation)

Let
t,u\in\Lambda_{\oc}
, and
\mathcal{R}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt sV}\}
. If
t\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}u
then there is
{\tt F}\in\mathbb{F}
such that
t^{\tt v}\rightarrow_{{\tt F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}^{\tt v}\right>}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}u^{\tt v}
, where
{\tt F}
and all contexts used for
the steps in
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
can be specified using

{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}^{\tt v},\mathcal{R}
and
t
.

Let us see how
{\tt F}
and the contexts used in the steps

\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
are constructed: it highlights the difference
between
Lem.

5
for
dCBV
and

Lem.

2
for
dCBN
.

•

Additional administrative steps (
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
) may be needed
at the end. For example, for the
dCBV
steps
u_{0}\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}u_{1}
and
u_{2}\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<{\tt sV}\right>}u_{3}
seen in
(
1
), we have:

\displaystyle u_{0}^{\tt v}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{der}\big(\big(\lambda x.\oc\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(y\oc x))\,\oc x)\big)\,\mathsf{der}((\lambda z.\oc\oc z)\oc y)\big)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\mathsf{der}\big(\,\oc\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(y\oc x))\,\oc x){\,[x\backslash\mathsf{der}((\lambda z.\oc\oc z)\oc y)]}\,\big)=s^{\prime}

(2)

\displaystyle\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(y\oc x))\,\oc x){\,[x\backslash\mathsf{der}((\lambda z.\oc\oc z)\oc y)]}=u_{1}^{\tt v}

\displaystyle u_{2}^{\tt v}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{der}\big(\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(y\oc x))\,\oc x\big){[x\backslash\oc y]}\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\mathsf{der}\big(\mathsf{der}(\mathsf{der}(y\,\oc y))\,\oc y\big)=u_{3}^{\tt v}

•

In
dCBN
one-step simulation the rule name and context are
independently translated. It is slightly more subtle in
dCBV
:
the rule name translates to the corresponding one in
dBANG
without any ambiguity, yet the translation of the context

{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}
depends not only on the initial context
{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}

but also on the rule name
\mathcal{R}
and the initial term
t
. Two
distinct situations can emerge:

–

{\tt dB}
-steps require to add a dereliction to the
translated context: for example, the
{\tt dB}
-redex
position
\diamond
in
t=(\lambda x.x)y
needs to be
translated to the redex position
\mathsf{der}(\diamond)
in

t^{\tt v}=\mathsf{der}((\lambda x.\oc x)\oc y)
.

–

{\tt sV}
-steps may need to remove a dereliction from
the translated context: for instance, the
{\tt sV}
-redex
position
\diamond\,y
in
t=(\lambda z.x)[x\backslash y]\,y
is translated to the
redex position
\mathsf{der}(\diamond\,\oc y)
in

t^{\tt v}=\mathsf{der}((\lambda z.\oc x)[x\backslash\oc y]\,(\oc y))
.
The context translation anticipates the
super-development used in
t^{\tt v}
.

Note that both situations can be detected by case-analysis on

\mathcal{R}
and
t
, where the target context translation is a slight
variation over the original one.

While the
dCBV
embedding
\cdot^{{\tt v_{2}}}
used in

[
17
,
18
]
successfully enables the simulation of
dCBV
into
dBANG
, it falls short when it comes to reverse simulation, as
shown in
Fig.

1
.
Therefore,
\cdot^{{\tt v_{2}}}
cannot be used to transfer dynamic properties from

dBANG
back to
dCBV
, thus failing in particular to derive

dCBV
factorization from
dBANG
(
Sec.

5
).
Our new embedding instead satisfies reverse simulation.

Lemma 6
(
dCBV
One-Step Reverse Simulation)

Let
t\in\Lambda
,
u^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\oc}
,
{\tt F}\in\mathbb{F}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt s\oc},{\tt d\oc}\}
.
If
u^{\prime}
is a
\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
, then

t^{\tt v}\rightarrow_{{\tt F}\!\left<\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\right>}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}u^{\prime}\ \implies\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\exists\;u\in\Lambda,&u^{\tt v}=u^{\prime}\\
\exists\;\mathcal{R}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt sV}\},&\mathcal{R}^{\tt v}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\\
\exists\;{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}},\end{array}\right\}\text{
such that }t\rightarrow_{{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}u.

Lem.

6
states that any
dBANG
diligent step from the image
t^{\tt v}
of a
dCBV
term
t

actually simulates a
dCBV
step from
t
. As expected, the same
subtleties encountered in the
dCBV
one-step simulation (
Lem.

5
) apply in
this last result, in particular regarding the construction of

{\tt F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}
. In the
dCBN
case, the absence of administrative steps
renders all sequences from images of
dCBN
terms diligent, making
stability, normal form preservation and simulations direct
consequences of one-step simulation (
Lem.

2
) and
reverse simulation (
Lem.

3
). This is not the
case for
dCBV
, due to the presence of administrative steps in the
simulation process. Indeed, when simulating
dCBV
reduction within

dBANG
(
Lem.

5
), administrative steps
are performed as soon as they become available, thus constructing a
diligent sequence. Conversely, projecting a reduction step from

dBANG
to
dCBV
(
Lem.

6
)
requires a diligent step. However, in the case of sequences,
in contrast to one-steps, there is
no requirement for administrative steps to be correctly synchronized,
and this may lead to deviations from the embedding’s image,
significantly complicating reverse simulation. Fortunately, the
diligence presented in
dBANG
(
Lem.

1
)
resynchronizes administrative steps yielding sequences
that are easy to project.

Corollary 3

Let
t,u\in\Lambda
and
s^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\oc}
.

1.

(Stability)
:
if
t^{\tt v}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}}s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
is a
\mathbb{F}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
,
then
s^{\prime}=s^{\tt v}
for some
s\in\Lambda
.

2.

(Normal Forms)
:
t\text{ is a $\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}$-{\tt NF} }
if and
only if
t^{\tt v}
is a
\mathbb{F}
-
NF
.

3.

(Simulations)
:
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}u
if and only if

t^{\tt v}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}}u^{\tt v}
. Moreover,
the number of
{\tt dB}/{\tt sV}
-steps on the left
matches the number
{\tt dB}/{\tt s\oc}
-steps on the
right.

As in
dCBN
, we may wonder whether similar preservation
results hold for surface reductions. Such results cannot be
entirely derived out from
Cor.

3
alone. Still, as
with
dCBN
, the
dCBV
one-step simulation and reverse simulation
properties
(
Lems.

5
and
6
)
already encompass the surface case. However,
even though surface redexes positions are mutually
mapped by the embedding, it does not yet imply surface stability,
preservation of normal forms, and simulations. As previously
explained, diligence is required to deal with administrative steps.
Fortunately, the surface fragment admits a diligence process, as illustrated in

Lem.

1
, which can then be
leveraged to obtain the following results.

Corollary 4

Let
t,u\in\Lambda
and
s^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\oc}
.

1.

(Stability)
:
if
t^{\tt v}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}}s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
is a

\mathbb{S}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>\!
-
NF
, then
s^{\prime}=s^{\tt v}
for some
s\in\Lambda
.

2.

(Normal Forms)
:
t\text{ is a $\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}$-{\tt NF} }
if and
only if
t^{\tt v}
is a
\mathbb{S}
-
NF
.

3.

(Simulations)
:
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt V}}}}u
if and only if

t^{\tt v}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}}u^{\tt v}
. Moreover,
the number of
{\tt dB}/{\tt sV}
-steps on the left
matches the number of
{\tt dB}/{\tt s\oc}
-steps on
the right.

Stability statements in
dCBV
(
Cor.

3
.1 and
4
.1) require
the reached term
s^{\prime}
to be normal for
{\tt d\oc}
, otherwise
stability does not hold (
e.g.
,
s^{\prime}
in (
2
) before
is not in the image of
\cdot^{\tt v}
), This is not required
in the
dCBN
stability statements (
Cor.

1
.1 and
2
.1) since
every term to which
t^{\tt n}
reduces is
\mathsf{der}
-free and
so
normal for
{\tt d\oc}
.

Proving simulation and reverse simulation requires a considerable
effort. However, this initial investment lays the groundwork for
numerous benefits without extra costs. For example,
in
Secs.

4
and
5
, we
demonstrate that typically challenging tasks like proving confluence
and factorization can be easily achieved through simulations. This
approach not only unifies the proofs but also minimizes the workload
for future proofs.

4
Confluence

Confluence is a crucial property in the
\lambda
-calculus, ensuring
that expressions consistently produce a single result, regardless of
the chosen reduction path. In this section, we examine confluence
across different reduction relations (surface and full) and within
various calculus frameworks:
dCBN
,
dCBV
, and
dBANG
. We
specifically leverage simulation and reverse simulation properties to
seamlessly project these results from
dBANG
to
dCBN
and

dCBV
, providing a comprehensive solution across three contexts.

Surface confluence is usually proved by showing that surface reduction
is diamond, as for example
in
[
17
,
18
]
.
Full confluence is more complex, as the full reduction relation is not
diamond, as one can easily notice with the term

(\lambda x.y)\oc\Omega
. Alternative
techniques
[
49
,
40
]
can establish full reduction’s
confluence, albeit often requiring numerous commutation diagrams and
potentially non-trivial bounding measures.

Theorem 4.1
(
dBANG
Confluence)

1.

(Surface)
The reduction relation
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}
is
confluent.

Moreover, any two different surface reduction paths to a

\mathbb{S}
-normal form have the same length and number of

{\tt dB}
,
{\tt s\oc}
and
{\tt d\oc}
-steps.

2.

(Full)
The reduction
relation
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}
is confluent.

Proof

(Surface)

See
[
17
,
18
]

(Full)
See
[
31
]
.

These proofs are typically highly technical, requiring a significant
amount of time to write and verify, and are prone to errors.
Therefore, it is extremely beneficial to have a method to streamline
them, especially when mecanising proofs. With the robust preservation
of
dCBN
reductions in the
dBANG
, we can actually project

dCBN
confluences directly from that of
dBANG
.

5
Factorization

In
\lambda
-calculi, reduction is a relation, so different reduction
steps are possible starting from the same term. Some steps (
e.g.
head
steps) are more significant than others, and they may occur in the
middle of a reduction sequence.
Factorization is the process of disentangling the significant steps
from the “superfluous” ones, bringing the former forward and leaving
the start and end terms unchanged.

This section is devoted to the factorization property for
dBANG
,

dCBN
and
dCBV
. We start by revisiting an abstract
factorization theorem
[
2
]
. We first apply this abstract
method to
dBANG
, thus obtaining a new result of factorization not
previously appearing in the literature. Then, we use the properties of
simulation and reverse simulation proved in

Sec.

3
to project the factorization result for

dBANG
into
dCBN
and
dCBV
. Although these two results can
be directly derived from the abstract factorization
theorem
[
2
]
, our approach circumvents the numerous
commutation properties required by the abstract approach. Also, it
provides a tangible illustration of how the simulation and reverse
simulation properties discussed in

Secs.

3.1
and
3.2

can be applied in concrete cases.

Abstract Factorization.

We recall an abstract factorization method from
[
2
]

that relies on local rewrite conditions given by the notion of

square factorization system
(SFS). While its original
presentation concerns only two subreductions, we (straightforwardly)
extend the notion of SFS to a
family
of subreductions, as in

dBANG
the reduction consists of more than two subreductions.

Definition 3

Let
\mathcal{R}=(R,\,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}})
be an abstract
rewriting system The family
\left(\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\circ}},\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\bullet}}\right)_{k\in K}
of paired reduction relations
is a
square factorization system (SFS)
for

\mathcal{R}
if it covers the reduction relation (
i.e.

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}}\;=\bigcup_{k\in K}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}}
where

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\circ}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\bullet}}
) and
satisfies the following conditions:

1.

Termination:

\forall\,k\in K
,
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\circ}}
is terminating.

2.

Row-swaps:

\forall\,k\in K
,
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\bullet}}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\circ}}\;\subseteq\;\rightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\circ}}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\bullet}}
.

3.

Diagonal-swaps:

\forall\,k_{1},k_{2}\in K
,
k_{1}\neq k_{2}
,

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k_{1}}^{\bullet}}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k_{2}}^{\circ}}\;\subseteq\;\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k_{2}}^{\circ}}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}_{k_{1}}}
​.

The symbol
\circ
tags
significant
(also called

external
) steps, while
\bullet
is used for
irrelevant

(also called
internal
) ones.
The commutations required in an
SFS are sufficient to achieve factorization, which consists in
rearranging a reduction sequence to prioritize significant steps

\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\circ}}
over irrelevant steps
\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}}
.

Proposition 1
(
[
2
]
)

Let
\mathcal{R}=(R,\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}})
be an abstract rewriting
system and
(\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\circ}},\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\bullet}})_{k\in K}
be an

SFS
for
\mathcal{R}
. Then the reduction relation factorizes,
that is,
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{R}}\ \subseteq\ \rightarrow^{*}_{\circ}\rightarrow^{*}_{\bullet}
where
\rightarrow_{\circ}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\in K}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\circ}}
and
\rightarrow_{\bullet}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\in K}\rightarrow_{\mathcal{R}_{k}^{\bullet}}
.

Factorization in
dBANG
.

In
dBANG
we claim that surface reduction is the significant part
of full reduction, and our goal is to factor it out. To exploit the
abstract method, we first formally identify the irrelevant subreduction of full reduction, called here
internal
, as
reduction under the scope of a
\oc
.

Internal contexts

{\tt I}\in\mathbb{I}
are full contexts
\mathbb{F}
for
which the hole is placed under a bang. Formally,

(
dBANG
Internal Contexts)

\displaystyle{\tt I}

\displaystyle\;\Coloneqq\;\oc{\tt F}\;|\;{\tt S}^{*}\!\left<{\tt I}\right>

\displaystyle\text{with }{\tt S}^{*}\in\mathbb{S}\setminus\{\diamond\}

Clearly,
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{F}\setminus\mathbb{S}
.
As usual,
\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>}
is the closure
of the rewrite rules
\mathcal{R}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt s\oc},{\tt d\oc}\}
over all contexts
{\tt I}\in\mathbb{I}
.
The
dBANG
internal reduction
is the relation

\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}}\,\coloneqq\,\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}\!\left<{\tt dB}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}\!\left<{\tt d\oc}\right>}
. For example,
(\lambda x.\oc\diamond)\,y
is an internal context while
\diamond
is not. Thus,

(\lambda x.\oc(z[z\backslash x]))\,y\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}\!\left<{\tt s\oc}\right>}(\lambda x.\oc x)\,y\not\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}}(\oc x)[x\backslash y]
.
We can now show that surface
and internal reductions enjoy the abstract properties of an SFS.

Lemma 7

The family
(\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>},\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}\!\left<\mathcal{R}\right>})_{\mathcal{R}\in\{{\tt dB},{\tt s\oc},{\tt d\oc}\}}
is an SFS for
(\Lambda_{\oc},\rightarrow_{{\tt F}})
.

This immediately gives the following novel factorization result for
the Distant Bang Calculus, by applying
Prop.

1
and
Lem.

7
.

Corollary 7
(
dBANG
Factorization)

We have that
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{F}}\;=\;\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{S}}\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathbb{I}}
.

Example 3

Take
t=(xy)[y\backslash\oc(I\oc\oc(Iw))]
where
I=\lambda z.z
.
Then, the
factorization of the first sequence starting at
t
below,
is given by the second one:

\displaystyle t\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}(xy)[y\backslash\oc(z[z\backslash\oc\oc(Iw)])]\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}x(z[z\backslash\oc\oc(Iw)])\rightarrow_{\mathbb{F}}x(z[z\backslash\oc\oc(z[z\backslash w])])\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}x\oc(z[z\backslash w])

\displaystyle t\;\;\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}\;\;x(I\oc\oc(Iw))\;\;\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}\;\;x(z[z\backslash\oc\oc(Iw)])\;\;\rightarrow_{\mathbb{S}}\;\;x\oc(Iw)\;\;{\rightarrow_{\mathbb{I}}}\;\;x\oc(z[z\backslash w])

Factorizations in
dCBN
and
dCBV
.

t
u

\scriptstyle\mathbb{F}_{\scalebox{0.55}{{\tt N}}}

(

)

(

:

)

t
